**What exists to build this from.** Per-rep targets are already a first-class concept — `budget_figures` has a `sales_person` dimension (schema/001), and the Performance screen (`performance_screen.dart`) already shows R Value/R Target/%Target per rep for a chosen fiscal month. Quote value by rep/period is queryable today via `sales_document_facts` (`document_kind = 'quote'`, filterable by `invoice_rep_code`).

**What's missing, and why it stopped the discussion.** `sales_document_facts` has no status/lifecycle field on a quote at all — no open/won/lost, no expiry, and nothing linking a quote row to the sales order it became (same gap already noted for the Quote → Order Conversion tile, Section 27). Working through what a "cover" number would actually need surfaced three unresolved questions, all guesses in the absence of real data:

- **Which quotes are "current."** With no status field, "open" can only be faked with a time window. Craig's inclination was a rolling 30-day window rather than tying it to the selected fiscal period.
- **Raw value vs. an assumed win rate.** Craig's call: show the raw quote value, no invented win-rate discount.
- **Double-counting.** A converted quote can't be told apart from a still-live one, so a rep's "cover" would double-count business already closed unless netted against their actual sales. Craig's call: net off actual sales for the same window.
- **What a 30-day rolling number even means next to a historical month.** The Performance screen lets you browse any past fiscal month, but a rolling-30-days-from-today figure has no relationship to a month someone might be reviewing weeks or months later — it would either need to go blank for any non-current month, or show a number that visibly has nothing to do with the rest of that row.

**Decision: not building this now.** Craig, once that last question was on the table: "I'm not sure that adding this right now makes any sense. There are so many anomalies that it becomes a guess with no substance. I think we should rather leave this until we have more concrete inputs." Right call — every rule on the table was compensating for missing data, not measuring something real.

**Prerequisite to revisit.** A status field on quotes (open/won/lost — or at minimum a reliable expiry/aging rule) coming through from whatever feeds `sales_document_facts`, ideally with a link from a quote to the order it becomes. Once that exists, a genuine coverage/shortfall figure is a straightforward build — no schema archaeology, no rolling-window guesswork, no netting heuristics. Until then, this stays a documented gap rather than a shipped feature built on sand.

**Files changed**: none — discussion only, no code touched.

## 37. Real CSV/PDF export, replacing the non-functional export buttons (2026-08-31)

Craig also asked "Is there anything else that you think should be included?" separately from Section 36. Five gaps were reported, ranked by severity — most urgent: the Export (CSV/PDF) buttons on 5 screens (Quote Analysis, Sales Order Analysis, Sales Analysis, YTD Comparative, and both dimension templates — Performance and Sales By) were pure stubs, wired to `DataExportButtons`, that only ever showed a "not implemented yet" SnackBar. Flagged as worse than having no button at all, since it looks like it works. Craig agreed this should be fixed first, ahead of the password-reset flow (the second gap on the list).

**Two decisions needed before building, both asked and answered directly.** (1) Deployment target — WyzeSales runs in the browser only (Craig: "In a web browser (Netlify URL)"), no native desktop/mobile build exists, so "export" only ever needs to trigger a normal browser download, never a native save-file dialog or filesystem permission. (2) Scope — CSV only first, or CSV+PDF together. Craig chose both together rather than staging it, so this round ships both formats across all 5 screens in one pass.

**What was built.** `lib/shared/widgets/data_export_buttons.dart` was rewritten from a stub into a real `DataExportButtons` widget: it now takes a required `Future<ExportData> Function() onExport` callback (headers + already-formatted display-string rows + a file-name base + a PDF title), builds a CSV via the `csv` package (UTF-8 with a BOM prefix, so Excel doesn't mis-guess the encoding on any accented character in a customer/item name), builds a PDF via the `pdf` package (`pw.MultiPage` in A4 landscape, so a long table paginates automatically rather than being clipped or squeezed), and saves either through `file_saver`, whose web implementation triggers exactly the ordinary browser download WyzeSales needs — no native dialog, no filesystem permission. A small `ExportUnavailableException` (clean `toString()`, no "Exception:" prefix) is thrown by an `onExport` callback for the two expected "can't export right now" cases — too many rows, or the data hasn't loaded yet — and shown to the user in a SnackBar rather than crashing.

Three packages added to `pubspec.yaml` (`csv: ^8.0.0`, `pdf: ^3.13.0`, `file_saver: ^0.4.0`), each with the same cautionary comment `data_table_2` already carries: exact SDK constraints couldn't be verified from this sandbox (no network access to pub.dev to run a real `flutter pub get`), so if the first real `flutter pub get` on Craig's machine hits a version-solving conflict — the same failure mode that made `data_table_2` need a downgrade to `^2.7.2` against his installed Dart 3.12.1 — send the exact error text back and it'll get pinned to compatible versions.

**Deliberately reused each screen's own on-screen formatting and totals math, rather than re-deriving it.** Every exported row uses the exact same `formatRand`/`formatPercent`/`formatQuantity` calls the table already shows on screen, and every totals row uses the exact same weighted-ratio recomputation each screen's own `_totalsRow` already does (e.g. %Target and %GP recomputed from summed Rand columns, not averaged from each row's own percentage — summing percentages that are each relative to a different base has no real meaning). Two screens (Sales Analysis' chart tab, YTD Comparative) had their month-grouping logic pulled out of the on-screen build method into a shared method also used by the export builder, specifically so the exported numbers can never drift from what's displayed — one calculation, two consumers, rather than two calculations that could quietly disagree.

**Per-screen wiring, screen by screen:**

- **Quote Analysis, Sales Order Analysis, Sales Analysis' Table tab** — all three share `lib/shared/widgets/document_analysis_view.dart`, whose table is server-side paginated (Section 22/23 — only 100 rows sit in memory at a time). Exporting the visible page would silently under-export any filter matching more than 100 rows, so the export callback does a fresh fetch of the *entire* filtered result set via the same paginated RPC (`page: 0`, a large `pageSize`), capped at 20,000 rows with a friendly `ExportUnavailableException` (not a crash) if a filter is broad enough to exceed that.
- **Sales Analysis** — its Chart and Table tabs share one export-buttons row above an `IndexedStack`, but each tab's data has a genuinely different shape. Solved with a "lift callback up" pattern: each tab (`_GraphTab`, `DocumentAnalysisView`) accepts an `onExportReady` callback, called once in `initState()` to hand its own export-builder function up to the parent screen, which dispatches to whichever tab is currently selected.
- **YTD Comparative, Performance, Sales By** — none of these three paginate; each already holds its full result set in memory (one dimension's rollup, or a fixed 3-fiscal-year comparison), so their export builders just await the same `Future` the screen itself is already showing, no extra fetch needed.

**Files changed**: `pubspec.yaml` (3 new dependencies), `lib/shared/widgets/data_export_buttons.dart` (full rewrite), `lib/shared/widgets/document_analysis_view.dart`, `lib/features/sales_analysis/screens/sales_analysis_screen.dart`, `lib/features/ytd_comparative/screens/ytd_comparative_screen.dart`, `lib/features/dimension_templates/screens/performance_screen.dart`, `lib/features/dimension_templates/screens/sales_by_screen.dart`.

**Verification**: all 7 files parse clean with the tree-sitter Dart check; delivered and byte-verified (sha256) against `/Users/craig/projects/wyzesales/...`. Craig needs to run `flutter pub get` before this compiles, to pull down the 3 new dependencies — see the version-solving caution above if that step reports a conflict. Worth Craig checking, on each of the 5 screens, that both the PDF and CSV buttons produce a browser download with the same numbers currently on screen, including the totals row, and that a Quote/Sales Order Analysis filter matching more than a page's worth of rows exports the *full* filtered set, not just the visible page.

## 38. Section 37's first real toolchain run — one real compile bug fixed, one one-time environment step identified (2026-08-31)

`flutter pub get` itself came back clean (no version-solving conflict, unlike the earlier `data_table_2` scare) — but `flutter run -d chrome` surfaced two separate issues, since this was the first time any of Section 37's code had touched a real Dart compiler rather than only the tree-sitter syntax check available in this sandbox (which parses Dart's grammar but can't catch a package API mismatch like this one).

**Real bug, in two parts: `csv` 8.0.0 doesn't have a `ListToCsvConverter` at all.** `data_export_buttons.dart`'s `_buildCsv` called `const ListToCsvConverter().convert(...)`, written from memory of an older `csv` package major version. The first symptom ("Not a constant expression," since that constructor isn't `const` in 8.0.0) looked like the whole problem and was fixed by dropping the `const` — but that fix was incomplete: the very next compile attempt showed `ListToCsvConverter` isn't defined at all in 8.0.0. That release replaced the entire class-based API (`ListToCsvConverter`/`CsvToListConverter`) with a `Csv` object exposing `.encode()`/`.decode()`, plus two ready-made instances — `csv` (standard settings) and `excel` (Excel-locale-specific settings, e.g. a different field separator in some locales). Re-fixed properly this time: `_buildCsv` now calls the package's own top-level `csv.encode([data.headers, ...data.rows])`, and the method's own local variable was renamed from `csv` to `csvString` so it can no longer shadow the package's `csv` instance the same way the original bug's naming invited. The `excel` preset was deliberately not used — the manual UTF-8 BOM this file already adds is its whole strategy for Excel compatibility, and layering an unfamiliar, undocumented delimiter default on top of that would be an unforced behaviour change.

**Not a bug: "This application is not configured to build on the web."** This project's repo has never had `flutter create` run against it for any platform — there's no `web/`, `android/`, `ios/`, or similar folder anywhere in the tree, because every file so far was written by hand rather than scaffolded. Since Craig confirmed (Section 37) WyzeSales only ever needs to run in a browser, the fix is a one-time step on Craig's machine, not a code change: `flutter create . --platforms=web`, run once from the project root. That backfills just the `web/` folder needed to build for Chrome/Netlify, without touching `lib/`, `pubspec.yaml`'s dependencies, or generating unwanted native (android/ios/macos/etc.) scaffolding — `--platforms=web` limits it to exactly the one platform this app targets.

**Third issue, found once the app actually ran: `PdfToBigPageException` on Sales Analysis' Table tab.** With the two compile errors above fixed, Craig could finally click Export — CSV worked, but PDF failed with "This widget created more than 20 pages." `pw.MultiPage` refuses past a `maxPages` limit that defaults to 20 (a debug-build-only safety net against a genuinely broken widget looping forever, per the `pdf` package's own docs — it isn't checked in a release build, but Craig was testing via `flutter run`, i.e. debug mode). Sales Analysis' Table tab shares `document_analysis_view.dart`, whose export re-fetches the *entire* filtered result set (Section 37) rather than one 100-row page — once real invoice data is behind it, that's routinely well past 20 pages' worth of rows. Fixed by explicitly setting `maxPages: 5000` on the `MultiPage` widget in `_buildPdf` — comfortably above what even `document_analysis_view.dart`'s own 20,000-row export cap could produce at this font size (roughly 40-50 rows per landscape A4 page), and free to set high since it's a page-count ceiling, not a memory pre-allocation.

**Fourth issue, found on the very next export: every em dash rendered as a tofu box.** Craig's YTD Comparative PDF came back with a box in place of the "—" in "WyzeSales — YTD Comparative (R Value)," and a box in place of every null-value cell (FY2027's not-yet-happened months, which `formatRand`/`formatPercent`/`formatQuantity` all render as "—" by the same shared convention — see formatters.dart). His browser console confirmed it exactly: `Helvetica has no Unicode support ... Unable to find a font to draw "—" (U+2014) try to provide a TextStyle.fontFallback`, repeated once per em dash on the page. The `pdf` package's built-in Base14 Helvetica/Helvetica-Bold fonts simply don't have a glyph for that character — and since every export title across all 5 screens follows the same `'WyzeSales — <Screen Name>'` pattern (or `'<Screen> — <Dimension>'` for Performance/Budgets/Dashboard), this wasn't a YTD-only bug, just the first one tested. The properly-recommended fix is embedding a real Unicode font — either `printing`'s `PdfGoogleFonts` (fetched over the network at render time) or a bundled TTF asset — but after three straight rounds of real-toolchain surprises from unverified packages already, adding a *fourth* new dependency for one known character wasn't worth it. Fixed instead with a small `pdfSafe()` string mapper inside `_buildPdf`, applied to the title, headers, and every cell right before they reach the PDF renderer, replacing em dash and en dash with a plain ASCII hyphen. On-screen and CSV output are untouched — Flutter's own font and Excel both render an em dash correctly — this is scoped to the PDF path only, where the actual limitation lives.

**Files changed**: `lib/shared/widgets/data_export_buttons.dart` (`_buildCsv` rewritten onto csv 8.0.0's real API; `_buildPdf`'s `MultiPage` given an explicit `maxPages: 5000`; `_buildPdf` now runs the title/headers/rows through a `pdfSafe()` dash substitution before rendering).

**Verification**: parses clean with the tree-sitter Dart check; delivered and byte-verified (sha256) against `/Users/craig/projects/wyzesales/lib/shared/widgets/data_export_buttons.dart`, four times now as each successive real-toolchain run surfaced the next issue — the incomplete `const`-only fix, the actual csv-API rewrite, the `maxPages` fix, then this dash substitution. This sandbox has no real Dart compiler and can't run the actual `pdf` rendering pipeline, so none of these — an API mismatch, a runtime widget limit, or a font glyph gap — could be caught ahead of time here; the tree-sitter check only validates Dart's grammar. Worth Craig re-exporting YTD Comparative's PDF to confirm the title and every null cell now show a plain hyphen instead of a box, and worth watching for the same "Unable to find a font to draw ... fontFallback" message on any *other* character in a customer/item name or elsewhere — if one turns up, send the exact text and it's a one-line addition to the same `pdfSafe()` map.

**Note on the standalone "Helvetica has no Unicode support" console lines.** Craig separately noticed these printing on their own, without an accompanying "Unable to find a font to draw..." line. Confirmed (via the `pdf` package's own issue tracker — several unrelated bug reports over the years have this exact two-line message as their entire content) that this is a generic, always-printed caution from using the package's built-in Base14 Helvetica/Helvetica-Bold fonts at all, independent of whether any specific glyph actually failed. Not an error on its own — only the character-specific "Unable to find a font to draw..." line (Section 38's fourth issue) means something didn't render.

## 39. wyzesales.com verified with Resend — outbound email now live (2026-08-31)

Closing the loop on Section 37's premise: Craig confirmed he already has a working `support@wyzesales.com` inbox hosted by Adept Internet, managed via cPanel — so the only remaining work was verifying the domain with Resend so it's allowed to send *as* that address, without disturbing the existing inbox's own mail routing.

Resend's current domain-verification flow (its dashboard, not the older docs' TXT+MX description) asks for three DNS records, all scoped to subdomains rather than the root: a `resend._domainkey` TXT record (DKIM — lets Resend sign mail as genuinely from `@wyzesales.com`), and two CNAME records, `rsend` and `send` (SPF/sending infrastructure, pointing at Resend's own mail-transfer hosts). Added via Adept's cPanel Zone Editor — one hiccup along the way: cPanel's TTL field rejected the word "Auto" with "The input should be a positive whole number," fixed by entering a plain number (e.g. 14400) instead. Resend's own "Enable Receiving" toggle was correctly left off — turning that on would have had Resend try to take over inbound mail (its own MX), which is exactly what Adept's cPanel-hosted inbox already handles and didn't need touching.

**Result**: Craig confirmed Resend shows the domain as fully verified. No code changes were needed — `send-upgrade-request` and every other Edge Function were already written against `support@wyzesales.com` (see Section 34's own notes on this), so outbound mail should now simply work.

**Files changed**: none — external DNS/dashboard configuration only, no code touched.

## 40. Password-reset flow — a real landing screen, plus an admin "send reset" button (2026-08-31)

Second of the two gaps Craig picked to fix after the export buttons: "password-reset flow is half-built — sends email, no landing page to complete reset, admin can't reset a user's password either." Both halves addressed in this round.

**Half one: the missing landing page.** `AuthRepository.sendPasswordResetEmail` called `supabase.auth.resetPasswordForEmail(email)` with no `redirectTo` — so Supabase sent the reset link to whatever default Site URL is configured for the project. The Flutter web client's `detectSessionInUri` (on by default) still quietly turned that into a valid signed-in session on arrival, but nothing then asked the user to actually choose a new password — appRouter's existing redirect rule (`isLoggedIn && isOnLogin → '/'`, otherwise stay put) just let them fall straight through to the Dashboard with a live session, as if they'd typed a correct password. That was the entire bug: the link "worked" in the sense of logging someone in, but never did the one thing a password reset link is actually for.

Fixed with a new `ResetPasswordScreen` at `/reset-password`, and `sendPasswordResetEmail` now passes `redirectTo: '${Uri.base.origin}/reset-password'` so the email link actually points there. The screen shows a "Verifying your reset link…" spinner, then a set-new-password form once a session is confirmed (checked once at `initState` and again via an `onAuthStateChange` subscription, covering either timing), and a "this link couldn't be verified" message — with an 8-second timeout as a fallback for the case where Supabase's web client had already stripped the token/error out of the URL before this screen even built, leaving nothing to detect a bad link from directly. Submitting calls a new `AuthRepository.updatePassword(newPassword)`, which just runs `supabase.auth.updateUser(...)` against whichever session is currently active — the recovery session in this flow, but nothing stops it being reused later for an ordinary "change my password" option inside the app.

**Router change needed alongside it.** `/reset-password` couldn't be gated the same way `/login` already is (redirect to `/login` whenever `!isLoggedIn`) — a recovery link lands here as the very first navigation, and whether `currentSession` is already populated at that exact instant depends on how far `Supabase.initialize()`'s own URL-token exchange has gotten, which isn't something this sandbox can verify against a real timing-sensitive run. `appRouter`'s `redirect` now treats `/reset-password` as always-reachable regardless of session state (same category as `/login`), so there's no race to bet on.

**Half two: an admin can now trigger a reset for someone else.** A "Send password reset" icon (a padlock-reset icon, tooltipped) was added to `_UserRow` in Settings > Users, next to Edit — hidden for a platform-admin/Support row, same reasoning pause/resume and Delete already use (that login isn't this client's to manage). It calls the exact same `sendPasswordResetEmail`, just for the target user's own email rather than the signed-in admin's — after a confirmation dialog naming who it'll go to. Deliberately NOT built as "admin picks a new password directly for them" — that needs Supabase's Admin API and a service-role key, which must never ship inside a Flutter web client. Re-sending the same self-service email needs no elevated privileges at all: whoever is signed in when `sendPasswordResetEmail` is called is irrelevant, since the account that actually gets reset is only ever the one named by the `email` argument.

**One more thing Craig needs to do, outside the code — same shape as Section 39's Resend DNS step.** Supabase requires every `redirectTo` value to appear in the project's own allow-list (Authentication → URL Configuration → Redirect URLs in the Supabase dashboard) or the reset link will fail to redirect correctly. Craig needs to add his production WyzeSales URL there — e.g. `https://<his-netlify-domain>/**` (Supabase's redirect allow-list supports `*`/`**` wildcards, so one entry covers `/reset-password` and every other path) — and, if he ever tests this via `flutter run -d chrome` locally rather than the deployed Netlify site, a second entry for local testing such as `http://localhost:*/**`, since Flutter web's debug server picks a random port each run.

**Files changed**: `lib/data/repositories/auth_repository.dart` (`sendPasswordResetEmail` gained `redirectTo`; new `updatePassword` method), `lib/features/auth/screens/reset_password_screen.dart` (new file), `lib/core/router/app_router.dart` (new `/reset-password` route; `redirect` treats it as always-reachable), `lib/features/settings/screens/settings_screen.dart` (`_UserRow` gained a "Send password reset" button + `_sendPasswordReset` confirmation flow).

**Verification**: all 4 files parse clean with the tree-sitter Dart check. Neither `resetPasswordForEmail`'s `redirectTo` parameter nor `updateUser`'s shape have changed in living memory of `supabase_flutter`'s API (unlike the `csv`/`pdf` packages' recent surprises), so the API-mismatch risk here is much lower — but this sandbox still can't run a real compiler, so treat this the same as every other round: if `flutter run` shows anything unexpected, send the exact text. Worth Craig testing three things once the Supabase redirect-URL allow-list step above is done: (1) "Forgot password?" on the login screen end-to-end — request the email, click the link, confirm the new set-password screen appears rather than dropping straight into the Dashboard; (2) an admin's new "Send password reset" button in Settings > Users, confirming the named user receives the email; (3) that a stale or already-used reset link shows the "couldn't be verified" message rather than hanging on the spinner.

## 41. Section 40's real-world test — go_router crashed outright on the reset link (2026-08-31)

Craig added the Supabase redirect-URL allow-list entry and tried the "Forgot password?" link for real. First attempt just bounced back to `/login` (the allow-list entry hadn't been added yet at that point — expected, see Section 40's own note on this). Second attempt, after adding it, crashed with a red Flutter error screen: `Assertion failed ... uri.path.startsWith(newMatchedLocation) is not true`, thrown from inside go_router's own route-matching code.

**Root cause: Flutter web's default hash-based URL strategy collides with exactly what Supabase puts in the URL.** Without an explicit `usePathUrlStrategy()` call — which this app never had, an oversight from Section 40's own build, not something Craig did wrong — Flutter web routes off the URL's `#fragment` (`https://mysite.com/#/reset-password` is the shape it expects), leaving the actual path before the `#` irrelevant to the router. Supabase's recovery link puts its own token in exactly that same fragment (`.../reset-password#access_token=...&type=recovery`). The two collided head-on: go_router tried to parse Supabase's raw token string itself as if it were an app route, and its internal assertion caught the resulting nonsense location before it could do anything worse.

**Fixed by switching the whole app to real path-based URLs.** `usePathUrlStrategy()` (from `flutter_web_plugins`, a package that ships with the Flutter SDK itself — like `flutter_test`, not a pub.dev dependency, so unlike `csv`/`pdf`/`file_saver` there's no version to guess at here) now runs in `main.dart` before `Supabase.initialize()`. This makes go_router read its own location from the URL's actual path (`/reset-password`) and leaves the `#fragment` entirely to Supabase, resolving the collision at its root rather than working around it. `ResetPasswordScreen`'s own `Uri.base.fragment` check (for a used-up/expired link) is unaffected — that reads the browser's real URL directly, regardless of which strategy Flutter's router uses.

**One companion change this made necessary.** Under the old hash strategy, Netlify only ever needed to serve `/` — every route lived after a `#`, which browsers never send to a server, so the app's own router handled everything client-side no matter what. Under path-based routing, a direct load of any *other* route is now a real HTTP request for that literal path — clicking the password-reset link and landing straight on `/reset-password` is the immediate case, but this now applies to every screen (bookmarking `/sales-analysis`, or refreshing the browser while on `/settings`) — and without a server-side rewrite, Netlify would 404 all of them: there's no real file at `build/web/reset-password`, only `build/web/index.html`. Added `web/_redirects` (`/*  /index.html  200`) so Netlify serves `index.html` for every path and lets Flutter's router take it from there. This only matters once WyzeSales is actually deployed to Netlify — testing locally via `flutter run -d chrome`, Flutter's own dev server already falls back to `index.html` for any path regardless, which is why the crash was reachable locally at all before this file existed.

**Files changed**: `pubspec.yaml` (added `flutter_web_plugins`, an SDK package), `lib/main.dart` (`usePathUrlStrategy()` call added before `Supabase.initialize()`), `web/_redirects` (new file — Netlify SPA fallback).

**Verification**: `lib/main.dart` parses clean with the tree-sitter Dart check; delivered and byte-verified (sha256) against `/Users/craig/projects/wyzesales/...`, including `web/_redirects` landing directly in Craig's own `flutter create`-generated `web/` folder (never seen by this sandbox before now — a new file, not an edit to one already there). Worth Craig re-running `flutter pub get` (picks up `flutter_web_plugins`, an SDK package, so no version-solving risk the way the other three new dependencies had) and trying the reset-password link again — clean path-based URLs like `/reset-password` should now show up in the browser's address bar instead of `/#/reset-password`, and the whole flow (spinner → set-password form → success) should run without the assertion. Worth a quick sanity check on a couple of ordinary screens too (e.g. Dashboard, Sales Analysis) to confirm nothing else broke from the URL-strategy switch — every navigation still goes through the same `context.go(...)` calls either way, so it should be purely cosmetic (cleaner URLs) everywhere except this one bug it fixes.

## 42. Real-world testing surfaced two more environment issues, then a deployment overhaul (2026-08-31)

Testing the fixed reset-password flow surfaced two further, unrelated environment issues, which led to a bigger decision about how WyzeSales gets deployed at all.

**First: Supabase's default email service has a 2-per-hour limit.** Repeated "Forgot password?" testing hit `AuthApiException(message: email rate limit exceeded, statusCode: 429, code: over_email_send_rate_limit)` — Supabase's built-in test mailer caps out at 2 emails/hour before Custom SMTP is configured. Fixed by pointing Supabase's Auth email at Resend instead (Authentication → Emails → SMTP Settings: host `smtp.resend.com`, port `465`, username `resend`, password a Resend API key scoped to Sending-access-only on the `wyzesales.com` domain specifically — least-privilege, since Craig's Resend account also sends for SeaWyze and The Relationship Index). No DNS work needed beyond Section 39's existing domain verification. This also means WyzeSales' own auth emails now come from `support@wyzesales.com` properly, instead of Supabase's generic default sender.

**Second: a genuine PKCE/local-dev limitation, not a bug.** Even with the SMTP and redirect-URL fixes in place, a *reused* reset link correctly showed "couldn't be verified" (links are single-use — expected). A *fresh* link, tested via `flutter run -d chrome`, still failed the same way — landing cleanly on `/reset-password?code=...` (confirming the router fix, redirect allow-list, and SMTP were all now working) but never establishing a session. Root cause: Supabase's newer PKCE-style reset link pairs its `code` with a secret the browser stored locally at the moment the reset was *requested*; completing it needs the *same* browser session. `flutter run -d chrome` launches a fresh, throwaway Chrome profile on a new random port every single run, so if Craig requested the email, then stopped/restarted `flutter run` before clicking the link (as naturally happens between rounds of testing), the browser that opens the link has no memory of the request, and the exchange fails silently. Not fixable in app code — it's a property of testing a PKCE flow against an environment whose origin changes on every run. Confirmed this stops being a concern on any *stable* URL (a fixed staging or production site, or even the same still-running local session) — the browser's stored secret persists correctly as long as the origin doesn't change between the two steps.

**That naturally raised the question of how WyzeSales gets deployed at all**, since testing this reliably needs a real, stable URL. Craig asked to match however SeaWyze is deployed. Checked directly on Craig's Mac (not from memory): SeaWyze is a git repo (GitHub, `AppWyze/seawyze`) connected to Netlify, with a `netlify.toml` at its root — a default `[build]` block builds and deploys `main` to production, and a `[context.staging]` block builds the `staging` branch against an entirely separate staging Supabase project, so test data never touches production. A community build plugin (`netlify-plugin-flutter`) installs Flutter inside Netlify's own build environment so `flutter build web` can run there directly from a git push — no manual "build locally, drag the folder onto Netlify" step at all.

**Assessed before copying it wholesale** (Craig asked directly whether this was actually the right approach, not just "the existing approach"): the two-Supabase-projects-plus-git-CI shape is sound and worth adopting — real isolation for testing, real deploy history, no manual drag-and-drop. Two things adjusted rather than copied verbatim: SeaWyze's `netlify.toml` types the actual Supabase URL/key directly into the build command as plain text; WyzeSales' instead reads them from Netlify's own Environment Variables (not a security fix — the anon key is meant to be public regardless — just tidier, one place to update rather than duplicated across two build contexts). And SeaWyze has no `web/_redirects` file at all, meaning it's still running on Flutter's default hash-based URLs — copying that detail would silently undo Section 41's whole fix, so WyzeSales keeps its path-based routing and `_redirects` file, diverging from SeaWyze on this one specific point.

**What's been done so far**: WyzeSales' project folder on Craig's Mac is now a real git repository (`main` branch, matching SeaWyze's naming), with two commits — the initial snapshot of everything currently in the folder, and a follow-up adding `netlify.toml` (production + staging build contexts, `netlify-plugin-flutter`, env-var-based Supabase credentials, `staging` context needing no separate `APP_BASE_URL` define since WyzeSales already computes its own base URL at runtime via `Uri.base.origin` — see Section 40). Both committed under the `Appwyze <support@appwyze.co.za>` identity, matching SeaWyze's existing commit history. One hiccup along the way, self-corrected: the very first `git init`/`git add` attempt (run through the same remote-device bridge used for every file delivery this whole engagement) left a stale `.git/index.lock` behind, because that bridge normally can't delete files on Craig's Mac at all — caught immediately from git's own warnings, fixed by requesting one-time delete permission for the session and re-running cleanly, rather than leaving Craig to discover a broken repo later.

**What's still needed, and belongs to Craig rather than something this sandbox can do on its own**: create an empty GitHub repository (no auto-generated README/gitignore, to avoid a conflict with the commits already made) — matching SeaWyze, likely `AppWyze/wyzesales` — then push the existing local repo to it from Craig's own Mac terminal (not through the device bridge, since pushing needs his real GitHub credentials, which this sandbox has no access to and shouldn't). Create a second, separate Supabase project for staging, and replay this repo's existing numbered `docs/schema/*.sql` migrations against it in order, so staging starts from the same schema as production. Create a Netlify site connected to the new GitHub repo (authorizing Netlify's GitHub access is a browser/account step only Craig can do), then set `SUPABASE_URL`/`SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` in that site's Environment Variables twice — once scoped to Production with the existing production Supabase project's values, once scoped to the `staging` branch with the new staging project's values. Create and push a `staging` branch to trigger Netlify's first staging build, note its resulting fixed URL, and add that URL (plus the production Netlify URL, once known) to each Supabase project's own Authentication → URL Configuration → Redirect URLs — the staging Supabase project needs the staging URL, the production Supabase project needs the production URL; they shouldn't cross.

**Files changed**: `.gitignore` (replaced the minimal `flutter create`-generated version with SeaWyze's own, for consistency), `netlify.toml` (new file).

**Verification**: `git log` on Craig's Mac shows two clean commits on `main` with no stray lock files or leftover temp objects; `netlify.toml` byte-verified (sha256) the same way every other file in this project has been. The rest of this section is unavoidably outside what can be verified from here — it's Craig's own GitHub/Netlify/Supabase accounts — so the natural next check-in point is once he's created the GitHub repo and pushed, at which point this sandbox can pick back up (writing the exact `netlify.toml` values, helping replay the schema migrations against the new staging project, etc.).

## 43. Section 42's plan corrected against reality, then carried through to a working, tested deployment (2026-08-31)

Two facts came out during execution that changed Section 42's plan before any of it was actually wrong in code — just wrong in premise.

**First correction: there was never a second Supabase project.** Craig clarified that "Wyzesales-staging" — the project this whole rebuild has been built and tested against from day one — is the *only* Supabase project that exists for WyzeSales, and it holds no live business data; everything in it is development/testing data. So Section 42's plan (production project + separate staging project, two Netlify build contexts) was solving for an environment split that doesn't exist yet. Confirmed with Craig directly rather than assumed: he'll create the real production Supabase project later, once he's happy with the app, at which point *that* becomes production and the current database keeps serving as staging — matching SeaWyze's shape properly, just not yet. For now, the goal was narrowed to what was actually needed: one stable, deployed URL to test things like the password-reset flow against, since `flutter run -d chrome`'s fresh-profile-per-run behaviour was the actual blocker (Section 42). `netlify.toml`'s `[context.staging]` block was left in place, unused for now — it costs nothing sitting idle and is exactly what gets pointed at the real staging project later.

**Second correction: GitHub's own auth requirements, encountered pushing for the first time.** `git push` failed with `Password authentication is not supported for Git operations` — GitHub dropped plain-password auth for git over HTTPS some time ago. Fixed by having Craig generate a GitHub Personal Access Token (Settings → Developer settings → Personal access tokens → classic, `repo` scope) and use that in place of a password, with `git config --global credential.helper osxkeychain` set first so macOS remembers it and he isn't prompted again. (The earlier `remote origin already exists` message in the same attempt was harmless — the first half of the original two-line command had already succeeded on an earlier try; only the push itself needed fixing.)

**Then the GitHub repo push itself succeeded** — confirmed both locally (`main` tracking `origin/main` cleanly) and against GitHub directly (`git ls-remote origin` showing the same commit hash on both ends) — landing all three existing commits at `AppWyze/wyzesales`.

**Netlify site creation surfaced one more permissions wrinkle.** The first attempt to import `wyzesales` into Netlify showed "No repositories found" — Netlify's GitHub App was already authorized on the `AppWyze` account (from SeaWyze's own setup) but scoped to specific repositories, and `wyzesales` wasn't yet one of them. Fixed via Netlify's own "Configure Netlify on GitHub" link, which opens GitHub's installation settings directly, where Craig added `wyzesales` to the allowed-repository list. Site imported cleanly after that, landing at `wyzesales-staging.netlify.app` (Craig renamed it himself from Netlify's random default `phenomenal-froyo-0ad76f`).

**First deploy went green but the live site was a blank white screen.** Diagnosed by loading the URL directly and reading the browser console rather than guessing: an uncaught Dart exception on startup, matching exactly the explicit check this app's own `supabase_config.dart` throws when `SUPABASE_URL`/`SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` are missing. Root cause: a Netlify build succeeding doesn't mean the dart-define values were actually non-empty — `flutter build web` compiles fine either way, it only fails at *runtime* if the values baked in were blank. Craig confirmed the two environment variables had never actually been added in Netlify's UI at all, so this wasn't a stale-build/redeploy timing issue — they simply weren't there yet.

**One more wrinkle while adding them**: Netlify's UI warns that a key named `SUPABASE_URL` is "commonly used for sensitive credentials" and offers to mark it as a secret. Left unchecked deliberately for both `SUPABASE_URL` and `SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` — the anon/public key is *meant* to be public (it ends up baked directly into the compiled `main.dart.js` that ships to every browser, by design, same as it does for every Supabase web client), and marking it "secret" risks Netlify's build-time secrets-scanning detecting that exact value sitting in the build output and failing the deploy outright, treating expected behaviour as a leaked credential. The one credential that must never go in here at all — secret checkbox or not — is Supabase's `service_role` key, which was never at risk here since only the anon key was ever asked for.

**After adding both variables (unchecked) and triggering a fresh deploy, the site came up correctly** — confirmed directly by loading `https://wyzesales-staging.netlify.app` and reading the page rather than taking "Published" at face value: the real WyzeSales login screen rendered, no console errors, no dart exception. Craig then tested the actual end-to-end goal of this whole deployment effort — the password-reset flow, from a stable, unchanging origin instead of a fresh-profile local Chrome session — and confirmed it now completes successfully: request the email, click the link, land on the set-new-password screen, no PKCE failure. The environment-specific limitation identified in Section 42 is resolved for this URL, as expected.

**Files changed**: none — this section was entirely external configuration (Netlify site creation, environment variables, GitHub App repository access, GitHub PAT auth) plus verification; no application code changed.

**Current state**: `AppWyze/wyzesales` on GitHub, connected to a live Netlify site at `https://wyzesales-staging.netlify.app`, building `main` on every push, configured with the current (only) Supabase project's URL/anon key as plain (non-secret) environment variables, redirect URL added to that Supabase project's allow-list, password-reset flow confirmed working end-to-end against it. `netlify.toml`'s staging context and the second Supabase project it will eventually point to remain future work, deliberately deferred until Craig is ready to stand up real production — at which point today's setup becomes the template for it rather than needing to be rebuilt.

## 44. Real favicon/PWA icons, replacing Flutter's stock defaults (2026-08-31)

Craig noticed the deployed site's browser tab was still showing Flutter's generic stock icon rather than anything WyzeSales-branded — a `flutter create`-generated web scaffold ships its own placeholder favicon/app icons and never had a reason to be revisited until there was a real deployed URL to look at.

The app already has a real brand mark: `AppLogo`/`_SignalSparkPainter` (Section 27's work, per that file's own doc comment — a hex badge with a gold pulse line through it, drawn with Flutter `CustomPainter` calls rather than an image asset, so it stays crisp at any size with nothing to keep in sync). That's exactly what's shown next to "WyzeSales" on the Login screen and sidebar. The web scaffold's favicon/manifest icons need actual PNG files, though — a browser tab or a home-screen icon can't run Dart — so a small one-off script, `tools/make_icons.py` (Python + Pillow, not part of the Flutter app itself), re-draws the exact same hex+pulse geometry and colours copied directly from `_SignalSparkPainter`'s own 72x72 coordinate grid and `AppColors` values, at each size Flutter's web scaffold expects: `favicon.png` (64px), `icons/Icon-192.png` and `Icon-512.png` (both transparent background, matching how the in-app logo has no background of its own), plus `Icon-maskable-192.png`/`Icon-maskable-512.png` (solid white background, mark shrunk to ~62% and centered so it clears the ~80% "safe zone" every OS's launcher-icon mask respects — Android/iOS can crop a maskable icon into a circle, squircle, or rounded square, and anything drawn outside that safe zone risks being clipped off).

While in there, also fixed the generic `flutter create` placeholder text nothing had gone back to update since the project was first scaffolded: `web/index.html`'s `<title>`, `apple-mobile-web-app-title`, and meta description all said "wyzesales"/"A new Flutter project"; `web/manifest.json`'s `name`/`short_name`/`description` matched, and its `theme_color`/`background_color` were still Flutter's own stock blue (`#0175C2`) rather than anything from WyzeSales' actual palette. All now read "WyzeSales" with a real description, and the manifest's colours use the app's own navy (`#0A1620`, `AppColors.navyDeep`) and white (`#FFFFFF`, `AppColors.lightSurface`) — the same two colours the deployed app already uses for its own branding and light-theme background, so a browser or OS that tints its own chrome around the PWA icon (Android's app-drawer label background, a bookmarked-tab's theme colour) now matches rather than showing Flutter's leftover blue.

**Files changed**: `web/favicon.png`, `web/icons/Icon-192.png`, `web/icons/Icon-512.png`, `web/icons/Icon-maskable-192.png`, `web/icons/Icon-maskable-512.png` (all regenerated from the in-app logo's own geometry/colours), `web/index.html` (title/meta-description/apple-mobile-web-app-title), `web/manifest.json` (name/short_name/description/theme_color/background_color), `tools/make_icons.py` (new — the one-off generator script, kept in the repo so the icons can be regenerated the same way if the brand mark ever changes rather than hand-edited).

**Verification**: all 8 files byte-verified (sha256) against Craig's Mac before committing; the icons were also visually previewed (composited against both a light background and as their true transparent/maskable form) before delivery to confirm the hex/pulse shape and colours actually matched `_SignalSparkPainter`'s output, not just "some hexagon." Committed to git (`13ee443`) — still needs Craig's own `git push origin main` from his Terminal, same reason as every push in this project (this sandbox's shell doesn't share his GitHub credential). Once pushed, Netlify's next build of `main` will pick these up automatically — no separate action needed on Netlify's side. Worth Craig doing a hard-refresh (or private/incognito load) of `wyzesales-staging.netlify.app` afterward, since browsers cache favicons aggressively and a normal reload can keep showing the old one even after the new file is live.

**A note on deploy timing, following on from this section**: Craig asked whether committing changes forces an immediate Netlify rebuild, since he'd rather batch several changes into one deploy than rebuild on every small fix. Confirmed: Netlify only builds on `git push`, never on a local `git commit` — and one push, however many commits it carries, triggers exactly one build. So the natural way to batch is simply holding off on `git push origin main` until ready; nothing else needs configuring. From this point on, changes in this project accumulate as local commits (verified and documented the same as always) and wait for Craig's own push rather than being pushed immediately.

## 45. Cross-dimension filter narrowing — picker options grey out when they'd return no data (2026-09-01)

Craig: "When a filter is applied, that filter needs to apply to the other filters as well. e.g. If I filter on an Item and then I look up Customer in the Customer Filter, I should only be able to select a customer who has purchased that item. Maybe Customers who have not purchased the item should be greyed out in the filter. This applies to all filters but not the Top Bar search." Confirmed with Craig before building (two open design questions, both resolved toward the fuller option): Year/Month participate in this too, not just the 5 entity dimensions; and a greyed-out option stays selectable rather than being hard-blocked — greying is a hint that a combination looks empty, not a rule preventing it, since a user might know new data is coming or want to double-check.

**The gap, confirmed by reading the actual code first rather than assumed**: `entity_search_field.dart`'s picker dialog (the sole remaining place any of the 5 dimension filters get set — see Section 27/that file's own doc comment) queries each dimension's plain reference table (`customers`/`items`/`categories`/`branches`/`sales_reps`) directly, with zero awareness of whatever OTHER global filters are already active. Filtering to one Item, then opening the Customer picker, showed every customer in the system — including ones who have never once purchased that item — with no indication a chosen combination would return nothing.

**Built on infrastructure that already existed for a similar reason**: schema/011 (Section 18-era work) built `v_sales_cube_monthly`, a view with every dimension's code on the same row (sales person, customer, item, category, branch, plus fiscal year/month) specifically so cross-dimension rollup queries were possible. This request needed the same shape of data — "which values of dimension X actually co-occur with the currently active filters" — so rather than a new data pipeline, this is one more SQL function reading that same view: `fn_dimension_filter_options` (schema/017), which returns the set of entity codes for a given dimension that have at least one matching cube row under every OTHER active filter. The target dimension's OWN current filter value is deliberately excluded from its own query (matching how `fn_dimension_monthly_sales_filtered` already keeps `p_dimension` and its own `p_filter_*` slot independent) — picking a *new* Customer must never be constrained by whichever Customer is already active.

**Flutter side**: `ReferenceDataRepository.filterOptionCodes(dimension, filters)` calls the new function and returns `Set<String>?` — null specifically means "no other filter is active, don't grey anything," letting the picker skip the round trip entirely in the common no-filter case rather than making a query whose answer would always be "everything matches." `_EntitySearchDialog` (entity_search_field.dart) fetches this once per dialog open, in parallel with its existing initial browse-list search, and renders any non-matching row with muted text plus a small "No data" trailing tag — `onTap` is unchanged, so tapping a greyed row still applies it as the filter, per Craig's explicit choice on this.

**Deliberately left out of scope, both by Craig's own words and for structural reasons**: TopBarSearch (`searchAllDimensions`) is untouched — Craig was explicit this only applies to the dimension filter pickers, not the "jump to any entity" top-bar search, which has no business being narrowed by whatever's currently filtered. The Document filter (`_pickDocument`) is also untouched — a document number is a single transaction, not an aggregate dimension entity, and has no column on `v_sales_cube_monthly` to narrow against the same way; Craig didn't ask for it either, so it wasn't force-fit in.

**Files changed**: `docs/schema/017_wyzesales_filter_options.sql` (new — `fn_dimension_filter_options` + its grant), `lib/data/repositories/reference_data_repository.dart` (new `filterOptionCodes` method), `lib/shared/widgets/entity_search_field.dart` (`_EntitySearchDialog` fetches and applies the greyed-out styling).

**Verification**: both Dart files parse clean with the tree-sitter check; all three files byte-verified (sha256) against Craig's Mac, committed to git (`c677fff`) — held back from `git push`, per the batching decision directly above this section, until Craig has more changes ready to go out together. **This feature needs one manual step Craig has to do himself before it works at all**: running `docs/schema/017_wyzesales_filter_options.sql` against the current Supabase project's SQL Editor, the same way every other numbered migration in this project has been applied — this sandbox has no direct database credentials, so it can write the migration file but can't execute it. Worth testing once that's run: filter to a single Item on any screen, then open the Customer filter picker and confirm customers who've never bought that item show muted with a "No data" tag rather than plain black text, and that picking one anyway still works exactly as before.

## 46. A functionality audit, a 10-item roadmap, and its first item — rep/branch-scoped access control everywhere (2026-09-01)

Craig asked directly: "What else do you think is missing from Wyzesales now from a functionality aspect?" Answered from an actual read of the full decisions log and codebase (not guessed): no automated tests anywhere; a known, deliberately-accepted gap in `budget_figures`' RLS (see Section 5); no quote/sales-order lifecycle status in the schema (blocks pipeline/conversion reporting); no proactive alerting (budget variance, negative GP%); no data-load health indicator beyond a bare timestamp; plus lower-priority polish (saved filter presets, scheduled/emailed reports, wider comparison windows, accessibility). Craig said "let's tackle all of these" and added two more himself: (1) the fiscal year start month is hardcoded to March everywhere and needs to be a real per-client Settings > Company field; (2) the 3-fiscal-year/36-month data history window needs to become a 3-or-5-year choice, also in Settings > Company, flowing through to the WyzeSalesExtract program itself.

**Sequencing worked out and agreed with Craig**: fix the known Budgets RLS gap first (cheap, isolated) → the two new Settings > Company fields together as one foundational phase (since later features shouldn't be built against a fiscal model that's about to change) → targeted regression tests specifically around the fiscal/rollup logic just touched → the three business-value features in priority order (quote/order lifecycle, alerting, load health) → the remaining polish items last. Logged as a 10-item tracked task list so progress stays visible across sessions.

**Item 1 turned into something bigger and more important than "fix one RLS policy" once actually investigated** — worth documenting in detail since the final shape differs a lot from the first plan. Before writing any SQL, checked what `budget_figures_select`'s SELECT policy actually gates today: not just the Budgets screen (already correctly admin-gated at the Flutter layer per Section 5), but ALSO the Dashboard's whole-company and per-rep target comparisons and the Performance screen's "R Target"/"% Target" columns — both shown to every signed-in user today, not just admins. A blanket "admins only" policy change would have broken both of those screens for every non-admin user. Reported this back to Craig rather than just building the naive fix.

**That correction led directly into Craig's real ask**: not "lock budgets to admins," but a full statement of the access model he wants everywhere — "A User can only see their data and Performance no one else's across the entire application. A RegUser can only see theirs and their Region... Other users Admin etc. can see everyones... The User Level combined with [Rep code and Branch code] determine what a User can view," plus: Rep code and Branch code become mandatory fields (blocking Save) whenever a user's Level is User or RegUser.

**The good news, confirmed by reading every existing RLS policy rather than assumed**: the core rule Craig just described is already fully built for actual sales/quote/order transactions. `sales_document_facts_select` (schema/001 Section 10) already does exactly this — AdminUser sees everything, RegUser is scoped to their own branch code, User is scoped to their own rep code via the union rule from Section 3 (invoice rep OR the customer's assigned rep, so a rep's own legitimate work is never hidden from them just because a customer is formally assigned to someone else). That already covers every report built on top of it: Sales Analysis, Quote Analysis, Sales Order Analysis, Sales By, YTD Comparative, and the actual-sales half of Performance and Dashboard. Nothing needed to change there.

**What genuinely was missing**, confirmed the same way: `budget_figures`/`sales_forecast` had no rep/branch scoping at all (any signed-in user could read every entity's target/forecast, company-wide) — matching Section 5's own note that this was a conscious trade-off at the time, not an oversight. And the reference tables behind the filter pickers (`customers`, `sales_reps`, `branches`) were scoped only to "same client," so every level saw the full company-wide list in every picker, even though selecting something outside their real access already correctly returned zero report rows.

**Two design questions needed Craig's own call before writing SQL, both resolved toward the fuller option**: (1) Item/Category/Company-level budget & forecast targets have no natural rep or branch owner at all — an item can be sold by any rep at any branch — so Craig chose to hide those three dimensions entirely for User/RegUser rather than show a company-wide number that doesn't mean anything at their scope; (2) the filter pickers themselves should also be scoped, not just report data, for a more consistent experience (Craig: restrict pickers too).

**Built as schema/018**, three small `security definer` helper functions (`fn_customer_visible_to_rep` — the exact same union rule as `sales_document_facts_select`'s own 'user' branch, factored out; `fn_rep_sold_at_branch` / `fn_customer_sold_at_branch` — the branch/region analogue for RegUser), then five rewritten SELECT policies: `budget_figures_select`/`sales_forecast_select` (dimension-aware: Sales Person/Customer/Branch scoped per the rules above, Item/Category/Company hidden for User/RegUser), and `customers_select`/`sales_reps_select`/`branches_select` (scoped the same way; `items`/`categories` deliberately left open — same "no natural owner" reasoning). Every existing schema/006 policy being replaced had to be explicitly dropped first, not just added alongside — Postgres OR's every applicable policy together, so leaving the old "any client member" policy in place would have silently kept everything visible regardless of the new one.

**A subtle bug caught and fixed before it could ever ship**: making `customers_select` read `sales_document_facts` (to decide a User/RegUser's visibility) creates a two-way dependency, since `sales_document_facts_select` already reads `customers` (to look up `assigned_rep_code` for its own union rule). That's structurally the exact same class of bug schema/005 documents in detail — "infinite recursion detected in policy for relation X" — just a two-table cycle instead of `profiles` referencing itself directly. Fixed the same way schema/005 did: all three new helper functions are `security definer` with a pinned `search_path`, so their internal queries bypass the target table's own RLS instead of re-triggering it. Verified this doesn't just avoid an error but stays *correct*: `fn_customer_visible_to_rep`'s predicate is an exact mirror of `sales_document_facts_select`'s own union-rule subquery, so the customer row that subquery needs to look up remains visible under the new `customers_select` policy in exactly the cases where it needs to be.

**Mandatory Rep code/Branch code**: `_AddUserDialog` and `_EditUserDialog` (settings_screen.dart) both gained a `_repBranchRequired` check — Save is blocked with an inline error whenever Level is User or RegUser and either field is empty, and the field labels themselves switch from "(optional)" to a required `*` live as the Level dropdown changes. Deliberately does NOT also scope User-level access by Branch code, even though it's now mandatory alongside Rep code — `sales_document_facts_select`'s existing 'user' rule was never based on branch_code (a rep can legitimately sell across more than one branch), and extending that now would invent a new restriction Craig didn't ask for.

**One side effect worth Craig watching for, not a bug**: the Dashboard's Rep Target Attainment section currently fetches every sales person's budget figures in one call with no per-user restriction — after this migration, a non-admin viewing that section will only see figures for reps schema/018's rules make visible to them (their own row for User, their branch's reps for RegUser), which is the intended new behavior, but will look different from what a non-admin sees today.

**Files changed**: `docs/schema/018_wyzesales_scoped_access_control.sql` (new), `lib/features/settings/screens/settings_screen.dart` (both user dialogs).

**Verification**: settings_screen.dart parses clean with the tree-sitter check; both files byte-verified (sha256) against Craig's Mac, committed to git (`e51338b`) — held back from push per the standing batching arrangement (Section 42/45's note). **Needs the same manual step as every schema change**: Craig running `018_wyzesales_scoped_access_control.sql` in Supabase's SQL Editor before any of this takes effect. Strongly worth testing with a real non-admin (User and RegUser) test account before moving on to the next roadmap item — this is the highest-stakes change made in this project to date (it changes who can see what), and this sandbox has no way to run it against a real Postgres instance to confirm empirically.

## 47. Section 46's real-account test — a false alarm, a genuine Supabase login incident, and a re-confirmed design decision (2026-09-01)

Craig tested Section 46 by pointing his own `profiles` row at `level='user', rep_code='R01', branch_code='CPT'` and signing in. Sales Order Analysis, Quote Analysis, and a Sales-by-Sales-Person comparative table all showed rows for other reps (Johan Botha, R02, R03, R04, R05, "44") across multiple branches — "Doesn't seem correct." Since `sales_document_facts_select` (schema/001) had just been relied on as "already fully built and working" to justify not touching it in schema/018, this needed a real answer, not an assumption.

**Code audit first, before asking Craig to touch anything**: re-read `sales_document_facts_select` itself, confirmed no later migration ever adds a second, more permissive SELECT policy on the same table (Postgres OR's every applicable policy together, so a stray permissive one would silently win), confirmed every screen in question reads through a chain of `security_invoker = true` views/plain SQL functions with no `security definer` anywhere in that chain, and confirmed the Flutter app is built with the anon/publishable key, never `service_role`. Everything on the code side came back clean, which meant the gap had to be either in what's actually deployed, or in the live database itself.

**Getting there took most of the session.** Confirming what's actually live needed two things from Craig: `select relrowsecurity from pg_class where relname = 'sales_document_facts'` (true) and `select policyname, cmd, qual from pg_policies where tablename = 'sales_document_facts'` — but the second one coincided with Craig getting logged out of the Supabase dashboard entirely, `auth.supabase.io` stuck in an infinite redirect loop back to GitHub OAuth, surviving cookie clears, incognito, and a full machine restart. Root cause turned out to be a stuck GitHub OAuth grant — revoking Supabase's access at github.com/settings/applications and re-authorizing fresh cleared it. Separately, and confirmed via Supabase's own status page mid-incident, there was also a live, unrelated Supabase-side incident ("401 errors due to JWT rejections," Aug 28–29) that plausibly explains the "JWT access denied" error Craig saw right as he got locked out — a genuine coincidence of two problems landing in the same hour, not something either side of this project caused.

**Once back in, the actual policy text came back byte-for-byte correct** — exactly the `adminuser`/`reguser`/`user`-union logic schema/001 describes, ruling out "never deployed" or "silently overwritten" as the cause.

**The decisive test**: rather than keep debugging through the app/browser, ran the RLS check directly against the database — impersonating Craig's exact `auth.uid()` via `set_config('request.jwt.claims', ...)` + `set local role authenticated` inside a rolled-back transaction, then querying `sales_document_facts` directly. This matters because the Supabase SQL Editor normally runs as a superuser, which bypasses RLS entirely regardless of policy — a plain query there proves nothing about whether RLS actually restricts anything. The impersonated query came back with 715 total rows (not the full company's ~2,300+), split as `R01: 523, 44: 52, R03: 39, R05: 39, R02: 33, R04: 29` — genuine proof RLS *is* restricting R01's access; this was never a leak of unrestricted company-wide data.

**Why other reps' codes still legitimately appear**: two independent rules, easy to conflate. Who can *see* a row is `sales_document_facts_select`'s union rule (own invoices, or any row on a customer assigned to you, regardless of who actually processed it — Section 3's own reasoning: a rep's legitimate work is never hidden from them). Who gets *reporting credit* for a row is a separate function, `resolved_rep_code()` (schema/001): if a customer is flagged `attribute_to_assigned_rep`, that customer's sales always display under the assigned rep's code in the Sales Person column, regardless of who invoiced it. So a row R01 is fully entitled to see (their own invoice, or their own assigned customer) can still show a colleague's code in that column, because that's genuinely who the sale counts toward. Branch spanning DBN/JHB/CPT in the same screenshots is likewise expected, not a bug — the `user` level was only ever scoped by rep code, never by branch (only `reguser` is branch-scoped).

**Put back to Craig directly, now that he could see the real effect rather than just the design doc's description of it**: keep the union+attribution behavior as originally decided in Section 3, tighten visibility to invoice-only (a User only ever sees rows they personally invoiced), or keep visibility as-is but relabel the Sales Person column to always show the viewer's own code on their own restricted screens. Craig's answer: **keep as designed** — no change needed.

**Net result**: Section 46's access-control model is now confirmed working end-to-end against the live database and live app, not just reviewed as SQL on paper. Nothing further to build here.

**Still outstanding, not yet done**: Craig's own `profiles` row is still set to `level='user', rep_code='R01', branch_code='CPT'` — needs reverting to `adminuser` (rep/branch code back to `null`) once he's satisfied, per the original test-account instructions. RegUser hasn't been tested the same way yet. Both worth doing before fully closing this out, though the underlying policy logic itself is now confirmed sound.

## 48. Configurable fiscal year start month (task #90) (2026-09-01)

Craig: "We are assuming that a Client's Financial Year runs from March to February but this will not always be the case. We need to add a field to the Settings > Company Screen whereby we can Add and Edit this. Then the presentation of the data in the relevant screen / filters etc. will need to align with this field."

**The good news, confirmed before writing anything**: the hard part already existed, unused. `fiscal_year_settings` (client_id, start_month default 3, override_year) and the `fiscal_year(doc_date, start_month)` SQL function were both built per-client-parameterized all the way back in schema/001 — `v_sales_documents` already computes every row's fiscal year via `fiscal_year(f.doc_date, coalesce(fys.start_month, 3))`. Nothing server-side needed to change for a row's fiscal year to already respect a non-March start month; it already did, for any client with a row in that table. The gap was entirely that nothing had ever written to that table outside the schema/010 seed data, and nothing in the Flutter app had ever read it — `lib/core/constants/fiscal.dart` hardcoded `startMonth: 3` as a default parameter everywhere, and a `fiscalMonthOrder` constant was a fixed `['Mar',...,'Feb']` list.

**schema/019**: two RLS policies (`fiscal_year_settings_adminuser_insert`/`_update`) mirroring `clients_adminuser_update`'s existing `is_adminuser() + client_id = get_my_client_id()` gate exactly — no new helper functions needed, both already existed (schema/008). Insert is needed alongside update because a client created before this feature has no row yet (schema/001's own `coalesce(fys.start_month, 3)` already treats a missing row as "assume March," so a client's very first save here has to INSERT, not UPDATE something that doesn't exist). Also added a `check (start_month between 1 and 12)` constraint — harmless while only a trusted seed script wrote this column, worth having now that a real dropdown submits it.

**fiscal.dart's core rewrite**: the old `fiscalMonthOrder` constant became `fiscalMonthOrderFor({startMonth = 3})`, a function that rotates a fixed Jan→Dec base list to start wherever the client's actual start month is (startMonth=3 reproduces the exact old default list; startMonth=1 gives the plain calendar year). `calendarMonthStartFor` was updated to call it internally rather than the old constant. `fiscalYearFor` itself needed no change — it already took `startMonth` as a parameter, just never received anything but its own default before now. Added `fiscalStartMonthName(startMonth)` for Settings' own human-readable display ("March", not the bare number `3`).

**The wide part**: every call site across the app that used to reference the bare `fiscalMonthOrder` constant or call `fiscalYearFor(DateTime.now())` with no argument had to start threading the client's real start month through — 8 files, ~20 call sites (`global_filter_bar.dart`, `document_analysis_view.dart`, `dashboard_screen.dart`, `ytd_comparative_screen.dart`, `performance_screen.dart`, `budgets_screen.dart`, `sales_by_screen.dart`, `sales_analysis_screen.dart`). A new `fiscalYearStartMonthProvider` (`app_providers.dart`, plain `FutureProvider<int>`, same convention as the existing `lastDataUpdateProvider`) backs all of them via `SettingsRepository.getFiscalYearStartMonth()` — no `clientId` parameter needed, same convention `BudgetRepository`/`SalesRepository` already use, since RLS (`fiscal_year_settings_select`, schema/006) already scopes the read to the caller's own client and the table's primary key guarantees at most one row either way; defaults to 3 client-side exactly matching schema/001's own server-side `coalesce`. Every call site reads it as `ref.read(fiscalYearStartMonthProvider).valueOrNull ?? 3` (or `.watch` where already in a build method) — falling back to today's pre-feature default while the provider is still loading, so there's no visible flash of wrong data, just a brief moment of the old always-correct-until-now default. Where a screen calls `fiscalMonthOrderFor`/`fiscalYearFor` more than once (budgets_screen.dart's `_MonthTable`, sales_analysis_screen.dart's `_GraphTab`), the rotated month list is computed once into a `late final _months`/local field at `initState`, same pattern those files' own `_fiscalYears` already used, rather than recomputed on every call.

**Settings > Company**: the Company info card gained a "Fiscal year starts" row (reads `fiscalYearStartMonthProvider` directly, independent of the existing `Client`-model `_future` — separate table, separate read). `_EditCompanyDialog` gained a 1–12 month dropdown, seeded from the same provider via `ref.read(fiscalYearStartMonthProvider.future)` in `initState` (async, since this value doesn't live on the `Client` model the rest of the dialog's fields synchronously read from `widget.client`) and saved via a new `SettingsRepository.updateFiscalYearStartMonth(clientId, startMonth)` (an `upsert` keyed on `client_id`, needed for the same "no row yet" reason as the RLS insert policy above). Saving calls `ref.invalidate(fiscalYearStartMonthProvider)` afterward so every screen picks up the change immediately, app-wide, without needing a full reload.

**A real bug caught and fixed before delivery, not hypothetical**: `DropdownButtonFormField`'s `initialValue` is only read once, when its internal `FormField` state is first created — a later rebuild passing a different `initialValue` does not by itself change what's displayed. Every other dropdown in this file (e.g. the Level dropdown) only ever changes its value via its own `onChanged`, which the widget already tracks internally regardless of rebuilds, so this was never an issue there. This one is different: `_startMonth` starts at 3 and gets overwritten from an *external* async source (the provider read in `initState`) sometime after the dialog has already built once — without a fix, the dropdown would keep showing "March" even after the real value loaded and `_startMonth` was correctly updated underneath it. Fixed with `key: ValueKey(_startMonth)` on the dropdown, forcing a fresh internal state (and therefore a fresh read of `initialValue`) whenever `_startMonth` changes for any reason, including the user's own selection — a no-op there visually, since by the time it rebuilds `initialValue` already matches what they just picked.

**Deliberately unaffected by this migration**: `fiscal_month_label()` (schema/002) — it returns the literal calendar month abbreviation for a date (`Mar` for any March date, always), which is a locale-safety measure, not a fiscal-position label; fiscal ordering has only ever lived in the Dart-side `fiscalMonthOrder`/now `fiscalMonthOrderFor`. `WyzeSalesExtract`'s own `AppSettings.FiscalYearSettings` config class already exists .NET-side — not touched here, since the extract program doesn't need this Settings field itself (task #91, the 3-vs-5-year data window, is the item that actually reaches the extract program).

**Files changed**: `docs/schema/019_wyzesales_fiscal_year_start_month_settings.sql` (new), `lib/core/constants/fiscal.dart`, `lib/core/app_providers.dart`, `lib/data/repositories/settings_repository.dart`, `lib/features/settings/screens/settings_screen.dart`, `lib/shared/widgets/global_filter_bar.dart`, `lib/shared/widgets/document_analysis_view.dart`, `lib/features/dashboard/screens/dashboard_screen.dart`, `lib/features/ytd_comparative/screens/ytd_comparative_screen.dart`, `lib/features/dimension_templates/screens/performance_screen.dart`, `lib/features/dimension_templates/screens/budgets_screen.dart`, `lib/features/dimension_templates/screens/sales_by_screen.dart`, `lib/features/sales_analysis/screens/sales_analysis_screen.dart`.

**Verification**: all 12 Dart files parse clean with the tree-sitter check (`check_dart.py`); a project-wide grep after the edits confirms zero remaining references to the old bare `fiscalMonthOrder` constant or a bare `fiscalYearFor(DateTime.now())` call with no `startMonth` anywhere in `lib/`. All 13 files byte-verified (sha256) against Craig's Mac, committed to git (`9d70f71`) — held back from push per the standing batching arrangement. **Needs the same manual step as every schema change**: Craig running `019_wyzesales_fiscal_year_start_month_settings.sql` in Supabase's SQL Editor before Settings > Company's new field can save anything. Worth testing by setting a non-March start month and confirming Sales Analysis/Sales By/Budgets/Dashboard's month columns all re-order to match, and that Year filter defaults shift accordingly.

## 49. Configurable data history window: 3 or 5 years (task #91) (2026-09-01)

Craig: "We are only looking at 36 months (3 years of data). I think there should be an option in the Settings > Company Screen for either 3 or 5 years of data that we can Add and Edit. Once again, the relevant screens and filters will need to align with this as well as the Server Side Extract program." Built the same day as task #90 and reusing every convention it established — same table, same RLS coverage, same provider/repository/UI shape — since this is genuinely the same kind of setting (one client-level number, editable from Settings > Company, needing to reach both the app and the extract program).

**schema/020**: adds `fiscal_year_settings.history_years int not null default 3`, plus a `check (history_years in (3, 5))` constraint — deliberately a fixed two-value choice, not a general range like start_month's 1–12, because that's exactly what Craig asked for and it's all the Flutter dropdown ever offers. No new RLS policy needed: schema/019's `fiscal_year_settings_adminuser_insert`/`_update` policies already cover this column, since Postgres RLS is row-level, not column-level.

**fiscal.dart**: added `fiscalYearWindow(currentFy, historyYears)`, a small generator (`List<int>.generate(historyYears, (i) => currentFy - (historyYears - 1) + i)`) that replaces every hardcoded `[currentFy - 2, currentFy - 1, currentFy]` literal in the app with something that scales to any window length and always places the current fiscal year last — the same "current year last" shape every call site already relied on when the window was a fixed 3.

**Wired through the same four screens that build a multi-year comparison window**: `global_filter_bar.dart`'s Year filter dropdown (now offers however many fiscal years are configured, still newest-first), `ytd_comparative_screen.dart` and `sales_by_screen.dart` (both already scaled their column generation off `_fiscalYears.length` rather than a hardcoded 3, confirmed by reading `_buildExportData`/`_buildTable`/`_columnPositions` before touching anything — so threading `fiscalYearWindow` through their existing `_fiscalYears` field was the entire change needed), and `sales_analysis_screen.dart`, which needed three separate fixes: the `_fiscalYears` computation itself, a hardcoded "Trailing 3 fiscal years, monthly." label and matching export title (now built from `_fiscalYears.length`), and a `seriesColors` array that was a fixed 3-element list indexed up to `_fiscalYears.length - 1` — caught before delivery, not user-reported, since it would only have thrown a `RangeError` the first time a client actually chose 5 years. Replaced with `List<Color>.generate(_fiscalYears.length, ...)` that keeps the exact same fading-neutral → info-blue → teal progression for the default 3-year case and safely extends it (every year older than "second-to-last" reuses the same faded neutral) for 5.

**Settings > Company**: same shape as task #90's start-month field — a new `fiscalYearHistoryYearsProvider` (`app_providers.dart`), `SettingsRepository.getDataHistoryYears()`/`updateDataHistoryYears()` (an upsert, same "no row yet" reasoning), a new info row and a `_dataHistoryWindowDropdown()` widget in `_EditCompanyDialog` with the same `key: ValueKey(_historyYears)` fix task #90 needed for `DropdownButtonFormField`'s stale-`initialValue` behavior, and `ref.invalidate(fiscalYearHistoryYearsProvider)` on save so every screen picks the new window up immediately.
